I mean, I was still me, but the work got done because the system kind of did the organizing for me. But now... without that old ticketing system and the escalation calendar they had, I'm just totally lost. My days turn into these massive piles of sticky notes all over my desk. Just sticky notes, half-finished emails, chess club reminders, and parts orders. \n\nSpeaking of parts orders, I was trying to order a replacement motherboard last week, and the vendor's website was still using HTTP instead of HTTPS on their checkout page. Can you believe that? In this day and age! It's basically inviting a man-in-the-middle attack. I actually started an email to their webmaster about it. I spent like an hour writing up this detailed explanation of how they need to update their SSL certificates, which is probably one of those half-finished emails just sitting in my drafts right now because I got distracted looking up the newest encryption standards. The new protocols are fascinating, they use these elliptical curve algorithms that are practically unbreakable unless you have a quantum computer... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, because everything is just in these random piles now—the notes, the emails, the reminders—I honestly can't tell what has to happen first until something is already overdue. Without that system forcing me to look at what's next, it all just blends together.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you did okay at work before you lost your job two years ago, even though you still had the fidgeting and other issues. But I want to clarify—back then, before the job loss, were you also having the same kind of trouble concentrating and finishing tasks that you're describing now, or is that concentration problem something that got much worse after you lost that structure?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, the concentration thing... it's definitely worse now, but it's always been a struggle to keep my mind on a task long enough to finish it. It's just that now, it's what really pushed me to come see you. I just can't do it anymore, even when it's critical. Like, I'll start tracing a client's network problem, right? Looking for a packet loss issue or something. And ten minutes later, I realize I've reread the same log entries three times and I still don't know what they say. \n\nLog files are tricky, though, especially if they aren't parsed correctly. You get these massive text dumps, and if you're not using a good log analyzer—like, I used to use Splunk, which is fantastic, but it's so expensive for a solo guy. There are open-source alternatives, of course, like ELK stack—Elasticsearch, Logstash, Kibana. Setting up Logstash is a bit of a nightmare if you aren't comfortable with Ruby, but once it's running, the visualizations in Kibana are just beautiful. You can map out IP addresses geographically in real-time. I spent a whole weekend once just watching brute-force login attempts hit a honeypot server I set up, mapping where they were coming from. Mostly Russia and China, which isn't surprising, but there was this one persistent cluster coming from a server farm in the Netherlands... \n\nBut yeah, the logs. I just stare at them now. The words just blur together, and I have no idea what I just read, so I have to start all over again. It's exhausting.\n\nDoctor: When you were in high school or college, before you started working at that corporate job, were you having the same kinds of concentration problems—like rereading things and not retaining them—or did that mainly start happening more recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it goes way back. Way before high school or college. My mother actually still jokes about it—she says that back in grade school, my report cards always said the exact same thing. They'd say I was a bright kid, but that I never stayed in my seat, I was always blurting out answers, and I constantly lost my homework... even when I had actually done it! Which is the most frustrating part, right? Doing the work and then it just... vanishes. \n\nBack then, everything was on paper, of course. We didn't have cloud backups or auto-save. I remember when we finally got a home computer, it was this old Tandy 1000, and I had to print my assignments on this incredibly loud dot matrix printer. You know the ones, with the continuous feed paper and the little perforated holes on the sides? I used to love tearing those strips off. If you did it too fast, though, it would rip the actual page and you'd have to print the whole thing over again, which took forever because the print head had to physically strike the ribbon for every single character. It sounded like a tiny machine gun. We used to buy the replacement ink ribbons in bulk from this catalog... RadioShack, maybe? \n\nMan, I miss RadioShack. You used to be able to just walk in and buy a single capacitor or a spool of rosin-core solder if you were working on a weekend project. Now you have to order everything online and wait three days, and half the time the components are cheap knockoffs that fail under any kind of thermal load. I bought a batch of voltage regulators last month that were supposed to be rated for 12 volts, and they fried at 9. Completely ruined the breadboard I was testing them on... \n\nBut, uh... wait, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, school. Yeah, the not focusing and losing things, that's been on my report cards since day one.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school and middle school, before you even got to high school, were you actually having trouble concentrating and understanding what you read back then, or was it mainly just the fidgeting and blurting out that teachers were noticing?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, the teachers definitely noticed the fidgeting and the blurting out, obviously. It's hard to miss a kid who can't stay in his seat. But the concentrating part... you know, I honestly don't remember if I had trouble reading back then. I mean, I read a lot of manuals and technical guides later on, but as a little kid? I'm not really sure. I know I lost my homework all the time, but I don't specifically remember struggling to understand what I was reading. \n\nI did read a lot of sci-fi, though. Isaac Asimov, Arthur C. Clarke. The Foundation series just blew my mind when I finally got around to it. The idea of psychohistory—using mathematics to predict the future of large populations—it's basically what we try to do now with big data and predictive analytics, right? Except Asimov was writing about it in the 1950s! It's incredible how prescient some of those authors were. I mean, Arthur C. Clarke basically predicted the geostationary communications satellite. We wouldn't have modern GPS or satellite internet without that concept. \n\nSpeaking of satellite internet, I was trying to help a client set up a Starlink dish out in a rural area last month. The phased array antenna inside those things is just a marvel of engineering. It electronically steers the beam to track the low-earth orbit satellites without any moving parts. Well, there are motors to orient the dish initially, but the actual tracking is all electronic. It's so much better than the old HughesNet systems where you had a massive latency delay because the signal had to travel 22,000 miles up and 22,000 miles back down. Trying to do a video call on one of those old systems was like talking to someone on the moon... \n\nBut yeah, as for elementary school... I really couldn't tell you if the reading comprehension was an issue back then. I just don't remember.\n\nDoctor: When you were reading those sci-fi books as a kid—do you remember if you had to reread passages a lot to understand them, or did the words just stick with you the first time?\n\nPatient: You know, with the sci-fi books... I really couldn't tell you if I had to reread them or not. It's just a blur, honestly. I used to get them all from the local library, these really beat-up paperbacks with the yellowing pages and that specific old-book smell. \n\nYou know that smell, right? It's actually lignin, the polymer in wood pulp, breaking down and releasing volatile organic compounds over time. It smells a little bit like vanilla. I read a paper once about how researchers can use gas chromatography to date old books just by analyzing those specific compounds. Gas chromatography is fascinating stuff. You vaporize a sample and run it through a coiled column with an inert carrier gas, usually helium. \n\nThough helium is actually a non-renewable resource, which is crazy when you think about it. We just waste it on cheap party balloons, but it's absolutely critical for cooling MRI machines and manufacturing semiconductors. If we run out of helium, the entire tech industry is going to hit a massive brick wall. There's a facility down in Texas, the National Helium Reserve, that supplies a huge chunk of the world's helium, and the government was talking about selling it off a few years ago. It caused these massive price spikes for research labs and tech manufacturers. You'd think we'd be trying to capture and recycle every drop of it during the manufacturing process, but the infrastructure for that is incredibly expensive to retrofit into an existing fab plant...\n\nBut, uh... anyway, I just don't know. I really don't remember how my reading comprehension was back then.\n\nDoctor: When you were working at that corporate job with all the structure—back before you lost it two years ago—were you able to read and understand technical documents and emails on the first try, or were you already having to reread things multiple times like you're describing now?\n\nPatient: At the corporate job... no, I could actually get through the documents mostly fine back then. The really serious trouble, where I'm just staring at a page and nothing is sinking in, that's been happening for about a year and 10 months. Basically ever since I was fired and lost all the systems that used to keep me on track. \n\nWhen I was there, I had this incredible, highly structured setup at my desk. I wrote all these custom VBA scripts in Outlook to automatically parse incoming technical emails, highlight the action items, and route them to specific folders so I only looked at exactly what I needed to read. Visual Basic for Applications is such a dinosaur of a language, though. Microsoft has been trying to deprecate it for a decade, pushing everyone toward JavaScript APIs, but nobody wants to rewrite millions of lines of legacy code. \n\nYou wouldn't believe how many Fortune 500 companies are literally held together by Excel macros written by some guy who retired in 2008. It's a complete house of cards. One time, Microsoft pushed a security patch that disabled macros by default for files originating from the internet, which, you know, is a smart security move to stop ransomware payloads. Emotet was running rampant back then using malicious Word documents. But that update completely broke our entire HR onboarding workflow. We had like 300 new hires who couldn't get their network credentials because the script that generated them was suddenly blocked by the Trust Center. I was up for 48 hours straight writing a PowerShell workaround to bypass the block just so people could log in on Monday morning... \n\nBut, uh, anyway. Without all those custom scripts and the rigid structure of that office, everything just fell apart for me. So yeah, the really bad concentration issues have been the last year and 10 months.\n\nDoctor: Going back to elementary school and middle school, when you were losing your homework and getting sent to the principal's office for fidgeting—did your teachers ever say anything about you not understanding the schoolwork itself, or not being able to focus on reading assignments and tests? Or was it mainly just the behavior problems they were concerned about?\n\nPatient: Well, no, they didn't really say I wasn't understanding the material. Actually, my mother still jokes about it to this day—she says my report cards in grade school always said I was bright, but that I never stayed in my seat, I was constantly blurting out answers, and I somehow managed to lose my homework even when I had actually done it. \n\nShe actually kept a bunch of those old report cards in a shoebox in her attic. They were printed on that continuous tractor-feed paper, you know the kind? With the little perforated strips of holes on the sides that you had to tear off. Dot matrix printers. The sound of those things printing was just... iconic. Probably an old Epson MX-80 just screeching away. You know, you could actually program those old print heads to play music by varying the frequency of the pins striking the ink ribbon? There are videos of people making them play the Super Mario theme. \n\nIt's really fascinating how the technology evolved from that. We went from impact printing to laser printers in what feels like a blink of an eye. The electrostatic process inside a modern laser printer is basically magic when you think about it. You have a laser drawing an electrostatic image onto a rotating photosensitive drum, which then attracts the microscopic toner particles, and then it gets permanently fused to the paper with heat. That fuser roller gets incredibly hot, which is why paper jams in laser printers are such a nightmare to clear without burning your fingers. I had to fix a massive Xerox multifunction machine at an office last week where someone tried to print on overhead transparency film that wasn't laser-safe, and it just melted straight onto the fuser assembly. Completely ruined a two-thousand-dollar part...\n\nBut, uh, yeah. The report cards. They just said I was bright but couldn't sit still or keep track of my papers. I don't think they ever thought I couldn't understand the actual schoolwork.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school and middle school, before you lost your job, do you remember having to reread things over and over because the words just weren't sticking—like what you're describing happening now with those log files at work?\n\nPatient: Well, as a kid? No, I don't think it was like this. But what's happening now... that's actually exactly what brings me in here today. I just can't keep my mind on a task long enough to finish it anymore, even when it's really important. Like you said, with the log files—I'll start tracing a client's network problem, and ten minutes later I'll realize I've reread the same log entries three times and I still don't know what they say. \n\nAnd to be fair, network logs are a complete nightmare to read anyway if the client hasn't set up their formatting correctly. Most decent places use a centralized syslog server, like Splunk or maybe an ELK stack, to aggregate everything. Splunk is incredibly powerful, you can write these beautiful regular expressions to pull exactly the packet data you need. But their enterprise licensing model is entirely based on daily data ingestion volume, which is honestly just daylight robbery. I see companies filtering out half their router traffic just to save on licensing fees, and then when a breach actually happens, they don't have the audit trails they need to do forensics. It's like turning off your security cameras because the hard drives are too expensive. I was trying to explain this to a client a few weeks ago when they were getting hit by a DDoS attack—just a classic TCP SYN flood, nothing sophisticated, but they had zero visibility into which IPs were hitting them because they'd throttled their own logging...\n\nBut, uh... sorry, what was the question? Something about middle school? I totally lost my train of thought there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Splunk is incredibly powerful, you can write these beautiful regular expressions to pull exactly the packet data you need. But their enterprise licensing model is entirely based on daily data ingestion volume, which is honestly just daylight robbery. I see companies filtering out half their router traffic just to save on licensing fees, and then when a breach actually happens, they don't have the audit trails they need to do forensics. It's like turning off your security cameras because the hard drives are too expensive. I was trying to explain this to a client a few weeks ago when they were getting hit by a DDoS attack—just a classic TCP SYN flood, nothing sophisticated, but they had zero visibility into which IPs were hitting them because they'd throttled their own logging...\n\nBut, uh... sorry, what was the question? Something about middle school? I totally lost my train of thought there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

I think we just touched on this, but no, at that corporate job it wasn't a problem. I could get through documents and emails on the first try back then. Like I was saying earlier, this really serious trouble with having to reread things over and over... it's only been happening for about a year and 10 months. People think the cloud is just magic, but it's literally just someone else's computer sitting in a massive data center. And when Microsoft has a DNS routing issue in their US-East region, suddenly your entire company can't authenticate to read a single email. 

I tried to warn the VP of IT that they needed a hybrid failover setup, but nobody wants to pay for redundancy until everything is already down. It's like trying to buy fire insurance while your kitchen is actively in flames. Speaking of fires, if the dedicated HVAC unit in a server room fails, the ambient temperature can hit 120 degrees in under an hour. I once saw a switch rack get so hot that the plastic clips on the CAT6 cables actually fused to the ports. You had to pry them out with a flathead screwdriver... 

Wait, what were we talking about? Emails? Yeah, reading them used to be fine before I got let go.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Dr. [Name]. Thanks for seeing me. Well, what brings me in is... I just can't keep my mind on a task long enough to finish it anymore, even when it's really important. Like, at work—I'm a computer support specialist—I'll start tracing a client's network problem, and ten minutes later I realize I've reread the same log entries three times and still don't know what they say. It's so frustrating. Actually, it reminds me of this one time I was trying to fix my brother-in-law's router. He bought this cheap off-brand thing, and I told him, I said, \"You get what you pay for,\" but he didn't listen. He never listens. He's the kind of guy who thinks he knows everything because he read an article online once. Anyway, his dog kept barking the whole time I was there, this little terrier mix, super loud. I don't know how they live with that noise. But yeah, I just... I can't stay focused on anything I'm supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice that you were having trouble concentrating like this? Has this always been something you've struggled with since childhood, or is this something newer that's developed more recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's definitely been forever. It's not a new thing at all. My mother actually still jokes about it, how back in grade school my report cards always said I was bright but I never stayed in my seat. I was always blurting out answers, and I would lose my homework even when I had actually done it. Which is just the worst feeling, you know, doing the work and still getting in trouble. \n\nMy third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable, she was so strict about that. She wore these awful floral dresses, looked exactly like couch upholstery from the seventies. And she had this giant jar of peppermints on her desk, but she never gave them to anyone. Just kept them there to taunt us, I swear. I think they were probably stale anyway, they looked dusty. \n\nBut yeah, my mom still brings up those report cards every Thanksgiving. It's like her favorite story to tell when my sister's kids are running around. My sister has three boys, and let me tell you, her house is an absolute zoo. You can't even walk across the living room without stepping on a Lego. Have you ever stepped on a Lego barefoot? It is pure agony. I told her she needs to get them those storage bins, the ones with the little compartments, but she says they just dump them out anyway. So yeah, I guess it's always been a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that as a child you couldn't stay in your seat and were always moving around. Do you still find yourself fidgeting, feeling restless, or having trouble sitting still now as an adult?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. It's like I have this motor inside me that just won't shut off. When I'm sitting with a client trying to troubleshoot their setup, or even when I'm in my weekly chess club meeting, my leg is just constantly bouncing. Or I'm clicking a pen, over and over, or twisting a plastic cable tie around my fingers until somebody finally gives me that look, you know? That annoyed look. \n\nSpeaking of cable ties, you wouldn't believe how many people don't know how to properly manage their cables. I go into these offices and it's just a rat's nest under the desks. Fire hazard, honestly. I always tell them, \"Get some Velcro strips, they're reusable,\" but they just use those cheap plastic zip ties and pull them so tight they crimp the ethernet cables. And then they wonder why their network speeds are dropping. It's basic physics, really. You pinch the wire, you degrade the signal. I read this whole forum thread about it the other night when I was supposed to be sleeping. It was fascinating, actually, this guy did a whole stress test on different brands of CAT6 cables... but yeah, sitting still is pretty much impossible for me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned trouble concentrating at work and fidgeting during chess club meetings. Besides work and hobbies, how is this affecting other areas of your life—like your relationships, your ability to manage household tasks, or your finances?\n\nPatient: Well, it's pretty much a disaster everywhere else, too. Since I lost the structure of my old job, things have just completely unraveled. My reputation has taken a real hit—I’ve lost freelance clients because I just can't keep track of things, and I actually missed out on this senior service-manager role I really wanted. And because of all that, my wife and I have had to worry about money, which is incredibly stressful. People just don’t trust me to follow through anymore. \n\nWhich is so frustrating because I know the tech better than anyone. I mean, my wife, she tries to be patient, but she gets on me about the house, too. Like, last week I was supposed to fix the leaky faucet in the guest bathroom. I went to the hardware store to get a washer, but they had this huge display of those new smart-home water leak detectors. Have you seen those? They sync right to your phone. I ended up talking to the guy in the plumbing aisle for forty-five minutes about the Wi-Fi protocols they use. The guy actually didn't know much, I had to explain how a mesh network would be better for a large house if you're placing sensors in the basement. Basements are terrible for Wi-Fi, the concrete foundation just eats the signal. You really need a wired access point down there if you want reliable coverage. \n\nAnyway, I ended up buying two of the sensors, but I completely forgot the washer for the faucet. So it's still leaking. And my wife was... well, she wasn't happy. So yeah, the money, the job stuff, the house... it's all just falling apart because I can't stay on track.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned these concentration and restlessness issues have been going on since childhood. But I want to make sure I have the timeline right—were you having these same kinds of problems consistently throughout your childhood and teenage years, or did they get noticeably worse at some point, like when you started working or got married?\n\nPatient: Well, the underlying stuff, the fidgeting and the blurting things out, that's always been there, like I said. But it didn't become this... this absolute wrecking ball in my life until about a year and ten months ago. That's when I got fired from my corporate job. \n\nI was at that company for almost fifteen years, and they had this proprietary ticketing system that was, frankly, a masterpiece of micromanagement. Every task had a barcode, every barcode had a timer, and if you didn't close a ticket within the SLA, your dashboard would literally flash red. It was annoying, sure, but it kept me moving. I actually knew the guy who wrote the original code for that system back in the early 2000s. He was this brilliant guy, lived off Diet Mountain Dew and those little powdered donuts. He used to keep a whole stack of them on his server rack, which is a terrible idea because the powdered sugar gets sucked right into the intake fans. You'd open up a server chassis and it looked like it had snowed inside. We had to take compressed air to the motherboards once a month just to keep them from overheating. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, when I lost that job and started trying to freelance, I lost all those systems that forced me to stay on track. So for almost two years now, it's just been a serious, serious problem. Without that red flashing dashboard, I just... I just drift.\n\nDoctor: Before you lost your job about two years ago, when you were working at that corporate job with all the structure and the ticketing system—were you able to manage your work responsibilities reasonably well back then, or were you still struggling with the same kinds of focus and attention problems you're having now?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, back then I actually did okay. I mean, I was still me, but the work got done because the system kind of did the organizing for me. But now... without that old ticketing system and the escalation calendar they had, I'm just totally lost. My days turn into these massive piles of sticky notes all over my desk. Just sticky notes, half-finished emails, chess club reminders, and parts orders. \n\nSpeaking of parts orders, I was trying to order a replacement motherboard last week, and the vendor's website was still using HTTP instead of HTTPS on their checkout page. Can you believe that? In this day and age! It's basically inviting a man-in-the-middle attack. I actually started an email to their webmaster about it. I spent like an hour writing up this detailed explanation of how they need to update their SSL certificates, which is probably one of those half-finished emails just sitting in my drafts right now because I got distracted looking up the newest encryption standards. The new protocols are fascinating, they use these elliptical curve algorithms that are practically unbreakable unless you have a quantum computer... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, because everything is just in these random piles now—the notes, the emails, the reminders—I honestly can't tell what has to happen first until something is already overdue. Without that system forcing me to look at what's next, it all just blends together.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you did okay at work before you lost your job two years ago, even though you still had the fidgeting and other issues. But I want to clarify—back then, before the job loss, were you also having the same kind of trouble concentrating and finishing tasks that you're describing now, or is that concentration problem something that got much worse after you lost that structure?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, the concentration thing... it's definitely worse now, but it's always been a struggle to keep my mind on a task long enough to finish it. It's just that now, it's what really pushed me to come see you. I just can't do it anymore, even when it's critical. Like, I'll start tracing a client's network problem, right? Looking for a packet loss issue or something. And ten minutes later, I realize I've reread the same log entries three times and I still don't know what they say. \n\nLog files are tricky, though, especially if they aren't parsed correctly. You get these massive text dumps, and if you're not using a good log analyzer—like, I used to use Splunk, which is fantastic, but it's so expensive for a solo guy. There are open-source alternatives, of course, like ELK stack—Elasticsearch, Logstash, Kibana. Setting up Logstash is a bit of a nightmare if you aren't comfortable with Ruby, but once it's running, the visualizations in Kibana are just beautiful. You can map out IP addresses geographically in real-time. I spent a whole weekend once just watching brute-force login attempts hit a honeypot server I set up, mapping where they were coming from. Mostly Russia and China, which isn't surprising, but there was this one persistent cluster coming from a server farm in the Netherlands... \n\nBut yeah, the logs. I just stare at them now. The words just blur together, and I have no idea what I just read, so I have to start all over again. It's exhausting.\n\nDoctor: When you were in high school or college, before you started working at that corporate job, were you having the same kinds of concentration problems—like rereading things and not retaining them—or did that mainly start happening more recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it goes way back. Way before high school or college. My mother actually still jokes about it—she says that back in grade school, my report cards always said the exact same thing. They'd say I was a bright kid, but that I never stayed in my seat, I was always blurting out answers, and I constantly lost my homework... even when I had actually done it! Which is the most frustrating part, right? Doing the work and then it just... vanishes. \n\nBack then, everything was on paper, of course. We didn't have cloud backups or auto-save. I remember when we finally got a home computer, it was this old Tandy 1000, and I had to print my assignments on this incredibly loud dot matrix printer. You know the ones, with the continuous feed paper and the little perforated holes on the sides? I used to love tearing those strips off. If you did it too fast, though, it would rip the actual page and you'd have to print the whole thing over again, which took forever because the print head had to physically strike the ribbon for every single character. It sounded like a tiny machine gun. We used to buy the replacement ink ribbons in bulk from this catalog... RadioShack, maybe? \n\nMan, I miss RadioShack. You used to be able to just walk in and buy a single capacitor or a spool of rosin-core solder if you were working on a weekend project. Now you have to order everything online and wait three days, and half the time the components are cheap knockoffs that fail under any kind of thermal load. I bought a batch of voltage regulators last month that were supposed to be rated for 12 volts, and they fried at 9. Completely ruined the breadboard I was testing them on... \n\nBut, uh... wait, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, school. Yeah, the not focusing and losing things, that's been on my report cards since day one.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school and middle school, before you even got to high school, were you actually having trouble concentrating and understanding what you read back then, or was it mainly just the fidgeting and blurting out that teachers were noticing?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, the teachers definitely noticed the fidgeting and the blurting out, obviously. It's hard to miss a kid who can't stay in his seat. But the concentrating part... you know, I honestly don't remember if I had trouble reading back then. I mean, I read a lot of manuals and technical guides later on, but as a little kid? I'm not really sure. I know I lost my homework all the time, but I don't specifically remember struggling to understand what I was reading. \n\nI did read a lot of sci-fi, though. Isaac Asimov, Arthur C. Clarke. The Foundation series just blew my mind when I finally got around to it. The idea of psychohistory—using mathematics to predict the future of large populations—it's basically what we try to do now with big data and predictive analytics, right? Except Asimov was writing about it in the 1950s! It's incredible how prescient some of those authors were. I mean, Arthur C. Clarke basically predicted the geostationary communications satellite. We wouldn't have modern GPS or satellite internet without that concept. \n\nSpeaking of satellite internet, I was trying to help a client set up a Starlink dish out in a rural area last month. The phased array antenna inside those things is just a marvel of engineering. It electronically steers the beam to track the low-earth orbit satellites without any moving parts. Well, there are motors to orient the dish initially, but the actual tracking is all electronic. It's so much better than the old HughesNet systems where you had a massive latency delay because the signal had to travel 22,000 miles up and 22,000 miles back down. Trying to do a video call on one of those old systems was like talking to someone on the moon... \n\nBut yeah, as for elementary school... I really couldn't tell you if the reading comprehension was an issue back then. I just don't remember.\n\nDoctor: When you were reading those sci-fi books as a kid—do you remember if you had to reread passages a lot to understand them, or did the words just stick with you the first time?\n\nPatient: You know, with the sci-fi books... I really couldn't tell you if I had to reread them or not. It's just a blur, honestly. I used to get them all from the local library, these really beat-up paperbacks with the yellowing pages and that specific old-book smell. \n\nYou know that smell, right? It's actually lignin, the polymer in wood pulp, breaking down and releasing volatile organic compounds over time. It smells a little bit like vanilla. I read a paper once about how researchers can use gas chromatography to date old books just by analyzing those specific compounds. Gas chromatography is fascinating stuff. You vaporize a sample and run it through a coiled column with an inert carrier gas, usually helium. \n\nThough helium is actually a non-renewable resource, which is crazy when you think about it. We just waste it on cheap party balloons, but it's absolutely critical for cooling MRI machines and manufacturing semiconductors. If we run out of helium, the entire tech industry is going to hit a massive brick wall. There's a facility down in Texas, the National Helium Reserve, that supplies a huge chunk of the world's helium, and the government was talking about selling it off a few years ago. It caused these massive price spikes for research labs and tech manufacturers. You'd think we'd be trying to capture and recycle every drop of it during the manufacturing process, but the infrastructure for that is incredibly expensive to retrofit into an existing fab plant...\n\nBut, uh... anyway, I just don't know. I really don't remember how my reading comprehension was back then.\n\nDoctor: When you were working at that corporate job with all the structure—back before you lost it two years ago—were you able to read and understand technical documents and emails on the first try, or were you already having to reread things multiple times like you're describing now?\n\nPatient: At the corporate job... no, I could actually get through the documents mostly fine back then. The really serious trouble, where I'm just staring at a page and nothing is sinking in, that's been happening for about a year and 10 months. Basically ever since I was fired and lost all the systems that used to keep me on track. \n\nWhen I was there, I had this incredible, highly structured setup at my desk. I wrote all these custom VBA scripts in Outlook to automatically parse incoming technical emails, highlight the action items, and route them to specific folders so I only looked at exactly what I needed to read. Visual Basic for Applications is such a dinosaur of a language, though. Microsoft has been trying to deprecate it for a decade, pushing everyone toward JavaScript APIs, but nobody wants to rewrite millions of lines of legacy code. \n\nYou wouldn't believe how many Fortune 500 companies are literally held together by Excel macros written by some guy who retired in 2008. It's a complete house of cards. One time, Microsoft pushed a security patch that disabled macros by default for files originating from the internet, which, you know, is a smart security move to stop ransomware payloads. Emotet was running rampant back then using malicious Word documents. But that update completely broke our entire HR onboarding workflow. We had like 300 new hires who couldn't get their network credentials because the script that generated them was suddenly blocked by the Trust Center. I was up for 48 hours straight writing a PowerShell workaround to bypass the block just so people could log in on Monday morning... \n\nBut, uh, anyway. Without all those custom scripts and the rigid structure of that office, everything just fell apart for me. So yeah, the really bad concentration issues have been the last year and 10 months.\n\nDoctor: Going back to elementary school and middle school, when you were losing your homework and getting sent to the principal's office for fidgeting—did your teachers ever say anything about you not understanding the schoolwork itself, or not being able to focus on reading assignments and tests? Or was it mainly just the behavior problems they were concerned about?\n\nPatient: Well, no, they didn't really say I wasn't understanding the material. Actually, my mother still jokes about it to this day—she says my report cards in grade school always said I was bright, but that I never stayed in my seat, I was constantly blurting out answers, and I somehow managed to lose my homework even when I had actually done it. \n\nShe actually kept a bunch of those old report cards in a shoebox in her attic. They were printed on that continuous tractor-feed paper, you know the kind? With the little perforated strips of holes on the sides that you had to tear off. Dot matrix printers. The sound of those things printing was just... iconic. Probably an old Epson MX-80 just screeching away. You know, you could actually program those old print heads to play music by varying the frequency of the pins striking the ink ribbon? There are videos of people making them play the Super Mario theme. \n\nIt's really fascinating how the technology evolved from that. We went from impact printing to laser printers in what feels like a blink of an eye. The electrostatic process inside a modern laser printer is basically magic when you think about it. You have a laser drawing an electrostatic image onto a rotating photosensitive drum, which then attracts the microscopic toner particles, and then it gets permanently fused to the paper with heat. That fuser roller gets incredibly hot, which is why paper jams in laser printers are such a nightmare to clear without burning your fingers. I had to fix a massive Xerox multifunction machine at an office last week where someone tried to print on overhead transparency film that wasn't laser-safe, and it just melted straight onto the fuser assembly. Completely ruined a two-thousand-dollar part...\n\nBut, uh, yeah. The report cards. They just said I was bright but couldn't sit still or keep track of my papers. I don't think they ever thought I couldn't understand the actual schoolwork.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school and middle school, before you lost your job, do you remember having to reread things over and over because the words just weren't sticking—like what you're describing happening now with those log files at work?\n\nPatient: Well, as a kid? No, I don't think it was like this. But what's happening now... that's actually exactly what brings me in here today. I just can't keep my mind on a task long enough to finish it anymore, even when it's really important. Like you said, with the log files—I'll start tracing a client's network problem, and ten minutes later I'll realize I've reread the same log entries three times and I still don't know what they say. \n\nAnd to be fair, network logs are a complete nightmare to read anyway if the client hasn't set up their formatting correctly. Most decent places use a centralized syslog server, like Splunk or maybe an ELK stack, to aggregate everything. Splunk is incredibly powerful, you can write these beautiful regular expressions to pull exactly the packet data you need. But their enterprise licensing model is entirely based on daily data ingestion volume, which is honestly just daylight robbery. I see companies filtering out half their router traffic just to save on licensing fees, and then when a breach actually happens, they don't have the audit trails they need to do forensics. It's like turning off your security cameras because the hard drives are too expensive. I was trying to explain this to a client a few weeks ago when they were getting hit by a DDoS attack—just a classic TCP SYN flood, nothing sophisticated, but they had zero visibility into which IPs were hitting them because they'd throttled their own logging...\n\nBut, uh... sorry, what was the question? Something about middle school? I totally lost my train of thought there.\n\nDoctor: When you were working at that corporate job before you lost it, and you were reading through technical documents or emails—did you ever have to reread things multiple times because the information wasn't sticking, or was that not really a problem for you back then?\n\nPatient: I think we just touched on this, but no, at that corporate job it wasn't a problem. Which, sure, it sounds great on paper, but they completely botched the Azure Active Directory synchronization. People think the cloud is just magic, but it's literally just someone else's computer sitting in a massive data center. And when Microsoft has a DNS routing issue in their US-East region, suddenly your entire company can't authenticate to read a single email. \n\nI tried to warn the VP of IT that they needed a hybrid failover setup, but nobody wants to pay for redundancy until everything is already down. It's like trying to buy fire insurance while your kitchen is actively in flames. Speaking of fires, if the dedicated HVAC unit in a server room fails, the ambient temperature can hit 120 degrees in under an hour. I once saw a switch rack get so hot that the plastic clips on the CAT6 cables actually fused to the ports. You had to pry them out with a flathead screwdriver... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Emails? Yeah, reading them used to be fine before I got let go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Now you have to order everything online and wait three days, and half the time the components are cheap knockoffs that fail under any kind of thermal load. I bought a batch of voltage regulators last month that were supposed to be rated for 12 volts, and they fried at 9. Completely ruined the breadboard I was testing them on... \n\nBut, uh... wait, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, school. Yeah, the not focusing and losing things, that's been on my report cards since day one.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school and middle school, before you even got to high school, were you actually having trouble concentrating and understanding what you read back then, or was it mainly just the fidgeting and blurting out that teachers were noticing?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, the teachers definitely noticed the fidgeting and the blurting out, obviously. It's hard to miss a kid who can't stay in his seat. But the concentrating part... you know, I honestly don't remember if I had trouble reading back then. I mean, I read a lot of manuals and technical guides later on, but as a little kid? I'm not really sure. I know I lost my homework all the time, but I don't specifically remember struggling to understand what I was reading. \n\nI did read a lot of sci-fi, though. Isaac Asimov, Arthur C. Clarke. The Foundation series just blew my mind when I finally got around to it. The idea of psychohistory—using mathematics to predict the future of large populations—it's basically what we try to do now with big data and predictive analytics, right? Except Asimov was writing about it in the 1950s! It's incredible how prescient some of those authors were. I mean, Arthur C. Clarke basically predicted the geostationary communications satellite. We wouldn't have modern GPS or satellite internet without that concept. \n\nSpeaking of satellite internet, I was trying to help a client set up a Starlink dish out in a rural area last month. The phased array antenna inside those things is just a marvel of engineering. It electronically steers the beam to track the low-earth orbit satellites without any moving parts. Well, there are motors to orient the dish initially, but the actual tracking is all electronic. It's so much better than the old HughesNet systems where you had a massive latency delay because the signal had to travel 22,000 miles up and 22,000 miles back down. Trying to do a video call on one of those old systems was like talking to someone on the moon... \n\nBut yeah, as for elementary school... I really couldn't tell you if the reading comprehension was an issue back then. I just don't remember.\n\nDoctor: When you were reading those sci-fi books as a kid—do you remember if you had to reread passages a lot to understand them, or did the words just stick with you the first time?\n\nPatient: You know, with the sci-fi books... I really couldn't tell you if I had to reread them or not. It's just a blur, honestly. I used to get them all from the local library, these really beat-up paperbacks with the yellowing pages and that specific old-book smell. \n\nYou know that smell, right? It's actually lignin, the polymer in wood pulp, breaking down and releasing volatile organic compounds over time. It smells a little bit like vanilla. I read a paper once about how researchers can use gas chromatography to date old books just by analyzing those specific compounds. Gas chromatography is fascinating stuff. You vaporize a sample and run it through a coiled column with an inert carrier gas, usually helium. \n\nThough helium is actually a non-renewable resource, which is crazy when you think about it. We just waste it on cheap party balloons, but it's absolutely critical for cooling MRI machines and manufacturing semiconductors. If we run out of helium, the entire tech industry is going to hit a massive brick wall. There's a facility down in Texas, the National Helium Reserve, that supplies a huge chunk of the world's helium, and the government was talking about selling it off a few years ago. It caused these massive price spikes for research labs and tech manufacturers. You'd think we'd be trying to capture and recycle every drop of it during the manufacturing process, but the infrastructure for that is incredibly expensive to retrofit into an existing fab plant...\n\nBut, uh... anyway, I just don't know. I really don't remember how my reading comprehension was back then.\n\nDoctor: When you were working at that corporate job with all the structure—back before you lost it two years ago—were you able to read and understand technical documents and emails on the first try, or were you already having to reread things multiple times like you're describing now?\n\nPatient: At the corporate job... no, I could actually get through the documents mostly fine back then. The really serious trouble, where I'm just staring at a page and nothing is sinking in, that's been happening for about a year and 10 months. Basically ever since I was fired and lost all the systems that used to keep me on track. \n\nWhen I was there, I had this incredible, highly structured setup at my desk. I wrote all these custom VBA scripts in Outlook to automatically parse incoming technical emails, highlight the action items, and route them to specific folders so I only looked at exactly what I needed to read. Visual Basic for Applications is such a dinosaur of a language, though. Microsoft has been trying to deprecate it for a decade, pushing everyone toward JavaScript APIs, but nobody wants to rewrite millions of lines of legacy code. \n\nYou wouldn't believe how many Fortune 500 companies are literally held together by Excel macros written by some guy who retired in 2008. It's a complete house of cards. One time, Microsoft pushed a security patch that disabled macros by default for files originating from the internet, which, you know, is a smart security move to stop ransomware payloads. Emotet was running rampant back then using malicious Word documents. But that update completely broke our entire HR onboarding workflow. We had like 300 new hires who couldn't get their network credentials because the script that generated them was suddenly blocked by the Trust Center. I was up for 48 hours straight writing a PowerShell workaround to bypass the block just so people could log in on Monday morning... \n\nBut, uh, anyway. Without all those custom scripts and the rigid structure of that office, everything just fell apart for me. So yeah, the really bad concentration issues have been the last year and 10 months.\n\nDoctor: Going back to elementary school and middle school, when you were losing your homework and getting sent to the principal's office for fidgeting—did your teachers ever say anything about you not understanding the schoolwork itself, or not being able to focus on reading assignments and tests? Or was it mainly just the behavior problems they were concerned about?\n\nPatient: Well, no, they didn't really say I wasn't understanding the material. Actually, my mother still jokes about it to this day—she says my report cards in grade school always said I was bright, but that I never stayed in my seat, I was constantly blurting out answers, and I somehow managed to lose my homework even when I had actually done it. \n\nShe actually kept a bunch of those old report cards in a shoebox in her attic. They were printed on that continuous tractor-feed paper, you know the kind? With the little perforated strips of holes on the sides that you had to tear off. Dot matrix printers. The sound of those things printing was just... iconic. Probably an old Epson MX-80 just screeching away. You know, you could actually program those old print heads to play music by varying the frequency of the pins striking the ink ribbon? There are videos of people making them play the Super Mario theme. \n\nIt's really fascinating how the technology evolved from that. We went from impact printing to laser printers in what feels like a blink of an eye. The electrostatic process inside a modern laser printer is basically magic when you think about it. You have a laser drawing an electrostatic image onto a rotating photosensitive drum, which then attracts the microscopic toner particles, and then it gets permanently fused to the paper with heat. That fuser roller gets incredibly hot, which is why paper jams in laser printers are such a nightmare to clear without burning your fingers. I had to fix a massive Xerox multifunction machine at an office last week where someone tried to print on overhead transparency film that wasn't laser-safe, and it just melted straight onto the fuser assembly. Completely ruined a two-thousand-dollar part...\n\nBut, uh, yeah. The report cards. They just said I was bright but couldn't sit still or keep track of my papers. I don't think they ever thought I couldn't understand the actual schoolwork.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school and middle school, before you lost your job, do you remember having to reread things over and over because the words just weren't sticking—like what you're describing happening now with those log files at work?\n\nPatient: Well, as a kid? No, I don't think it was like this. But what's happening now... that's actually exactly what brings me in here today. I just can't keep my mind on a task long enough to finish it anymore, even when it's really important. Like you said, with the log files—I'll start tracing a client's network problem, and ten minutes later I'll realize I've reread the same log entries three times and I still don't know what they say. \n\nAnd to be fair, network logs are a complete nightmare to read anyway if the client hasn't set up their formatting correctly. Most decent places use a centralized syslog server, like Splunk or maybe an ELK stack, to aggregate everything. Splunk is incredibly powerful, you can write these beautiful regular expressions to pull exactly the packet data you need. But their enterprise licensing model is entirely based on daily data ingestion volume, which is honestly just daylight robbery. I see companies filtering out half their router traffic just to save on licensing fees, and then when a breach actually happens, they don't have the audit trails they need to do forensics. It's like turning off your security cameras because the hard drives are too expensive. I was trying to explain this to a client a few weeks ago when they were getting hit by a DDoS attack—just a classic TCP SYN flood, nothing sophisticated, but they had zero visibility into which IPs were hitting them because they'd throttled their own logging...\n\nBut, uh... sorry, what was the question? Something about middle school? I totally lost my train of thought there.\n\nDoctor: When you were working at that corporate job before you lost it, and you were reading through technical documents or emails—did you ever have to reread things multiple times because the information wasn't sticking, or was that not really a problem for you back then?\n\nPatient: I think we just touched on this, but no, at that corporate job it wasn't a problem. I could get through documents and emails on the first try back then. Like I was saying earlier, this really serious trouble with having to reread things over and over... it's only been happening for about a year and 10 months. Exactly since I was fired and lost all those rigid systems that used to keep me on track. \n\nThey actually let me go because they were moving the entire infrastructure to the cloud, you know? They brought in these outside consultants who promised the executives they didn't need on-premise server admins anymore. They migrated the whole company to Office 365. Which, sure, it sounds great on paper, but they completely botched the Azure Active Directory synchronization. People think the cloud is just magic, but it's literally just someone else's computer sitting in a massive data center. And when Microsoft has a DNS routing issue in their US-East region, suddenly your entire company can't authenticate to read a single email. \n\nI tried to warn the VP of IT that they needed a hybrid failover setup, but nobody wants to pay for redundancy until everything is already down. It's like trying to buy fire insurance while your kitchen is actively in flames. Speaking of fires, if the dedicated HVAC unit in a server room fails, the ambient temperature can hit 120 degrees in under an hour. I once saw a switch rack get so hot that the plastic clips on the CAT6 cables actually fused to the ports. You had to pry them out with a flathead screwdriver... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Emails? Yeah, reading them used to be fine before I got let go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

This distinction is critical: persistent inattention to reading since childhood supports ADHD, while new-onset reading comprehension problems after a major life stressor suggests adjustment or mood issues rather than primary ADHD.",
  "question": "Back when you were in high school or college, before you ever started that corporate job, do you remember having to reread textbooks or assignments over and over because the information just wasn't sticking in your head—like what's happening now with those log files?"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
In high school or college? No, I could read my textbooks just fine back then. I actually still have some of my old computer science textbooks from undergrad. They are absolute bricks. I remember my first real networking textbook was heavy on Novell NetWare and Token Ring topologies. You never see Token Ring anymore, which is a blessing. We had to wire the labs using this coaxial cable—Thinnet, 10BASE2—and you had to daisy-chain every single workstation together with these metal T-connectors. 

The worst part was that it was a bus topology, so if one student accidentally kicked the cable under their desk and broke the connection, or if someone stole the 50-ohm terminator off the end of the line, the entire room's network would instantly drop dead. We'd have to crawl under the desks with a multimeter just to find the break. The BNC connectors were a pain to crimp, too. If you didn't strip the copper shielding back perfectly, you'd get massive signal reflection and packet collisions... 

But, uh, anyway. Like I was telling you a minute ago, that's exactly the reason I'm sitting here talking to you today. I just can't keep my mind on a task long enough to finish it anymore, even when it's really important. Just like I said with those log files—I'll be trying to trace a client's network problem, and ten minutes will go by, and I'll realize I've reread the exact same lines three times and I still don't know what they say. It's incredibly frustrating because my brain used to work. But no, back in school, reading wasn't an issue at all.

We went from impact printing to laser printers in what feels like a blink of an eye. The electrostatic process inside a modern laser printer is basically magic when you think about it. You have a laser drawing an electrostatic image onto a rotating photosensitive drum, which then attracts the microscopic toner particles, and then it gets permanently fused to the paper with heat. That fuser roller gets incredibly hot, which is why paper jams in laser printers are such a nightmare to clear without burning your fingers. But their enterprise licensing model is entirely based on daily data ingestion volume, which is honestly just daylight robbery.
